⏺ All 6 existing tests still pass with the new cycle-batched design.

  Summary of the new ReactiveHelloWorldByteProcessor

  Fields:
  - bytePublisher — one shared ReactiveHelloWorldBytePublisher instance
  - pendingSubscribers — BlockingQueue<State> (a LinkedBlockingQueue)
  - fetcher — one dedicated long-running virtual thread

  Per-State:
  - downstream, queue (ArrayBlockingQueue<Byte> of capacity 12), demand, cancelled, lock, workAvailable, terminalError

  Three concurrent moving parts (as described in the class javadoc):

  2. One worker thread per subscriber, started in subscribe(...). The worker calls downstream.onSubscribe, then pendingSubscribers.offer(state) (wakes the fetcher), then loops on its own queue delivering bytes as demand
  permits.
  3. The byte publisher's producer thread inside each cycle's BytePublisher.subscribe(...) — self-terminates after 12 bytes.

  Key behaviors verified by the existing tests:
  - Single subscriber: gets all 12 bytes + onComplete.
  - Two equal subscribers, two different-pace subscribers: each gets 12 bytes — in this design, they may share a cycle if they cluster timing-wise.
  - Mid-cancel: cancelled subscriber stops; others complete.
  - Late-join: still receives its own 12 bytes (in this design, by triggering a new cycle).

  Caveats / known limitations:

  - The fetcher virtual thread runs forever; no close()/AutoCloseable yet. Virtual threads are daemons so the JVM still exits cleanly.
  - A cancel that arrives between subscribe() and the worker reaching pendingSubscribers.offer(state) is harmless — the cancelled flag is checked in distribute.
  - An error from the byte publisher mid-cycle is delivered to first and any pending subscribers via the terminalError field.

  Want me to also exercise the "cycle batching" behavior with a new test (e.g., assert that 2 subscribers issued back-to-back trigger only one service.set call, demonstrating the shared cycle)? That would prove the
  design's value isn't being hidden by the existing tests.

⏺ Yes, I read them now. Let me play them back so we're aligned:

  Inside subscribe(Subscriber<? super Byte> downstream), your TODOs sketch two responsibilities:

  Block A — per-subscriber side

  1. Build a State for this downstream and push it onto the outer queue.
  2. Start a per-state thread that waits for its own queue to be filled with all 12 bytes, then publishes them to downstream as request(n) demands, and exits after all 12 are served.

  Block B — outer / fetcher side

  1. Lazily initialize and start a single outer thread (only the first subscribe(...) triggers it).
  2. That thread subscribes this (the Processor itself) to the byte publisher and stores the subscription.
  3. When the 12th onNext arrives, cancel the subscription.
  4. Then drain the outer queue and push all 12 bytes into each pending state's queue.

  The key difference from my last implementation: this is the Subscriber<Byte> that the byte publisher gets subscribed to — so this.onSubscribe(...), this.onNext(...), this.onError(...), this.onComplete() are real,
  executed code, not dead UnsupportedOperationException methods.

  A couple of design points before I rewrite the class:

  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐

  ├────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Single-cycle or multi-cycle Processor?             │ Multi-cycle. Outer thread loops: take() first → subscribe this to byte publisher → wait for cycle → loop. (Each cycle calls this.onSubscribe(...) again; spec    │
  │                                                    │ Rule 2.12 strictly forbids that, but ReactiveHelloWorldBytePublisher doesn't enforce it, so it works.)                                                           │

  Read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ I see — you're migrating from the old design (inner anonymous Subscriber per cycle, eager fetcher) to the new design (this IS the Subscriber to upstream, lazily-started outer thread). The migration is partial right now.
  Here's what I see:

  What's in place (the new direction)

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────┐
  │                      Piece                       │             Status              │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ onSubscribe(s) stores upstreamSubscription = s       d                    │ ✅ done                         │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ onNext(b) accumulates into shared array[index++]     d    (    a          │ ✅ done (the accumulation part) │                                                                                                             ─
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ subscribe(downstream) lazily starts outer thread under synchronized(this) │ ✅ skeleton only                │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Shared cycle state: array, index, upstreamSubscription                    │ ✅ fields exist                 │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ statesQueue (per-cycle pending list)                                      │ ✅ field exists                 │
  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────┘

  What's missing or incomplete

  In subscribe(downstream) (lines 170-182)

  Only the lazy outer-start is sketched. Missing:

  1. Objects.requireNonNull(downstream, "downstream is null")
  2. Create a new State(downstream) for this subscriber.
  3. Call downstream.onSubscribe(new Subscription() { … }) — the Subscription whose request(n) flips state.demand+signal(state) and whose cancel() flips state.cancelled+signal(state).
  4. Start a per-subscriber worker virtual thread that:
    - waits on state.queue until items + demand are available,
    - delivers state.downstream.onNext(state.queue.poll()) while decrementing state.demand,
    - calls state.downstream.onComplete() after 12 delivered.
  5. statesQueue.offer(state) — push to the outer queue so the outer thread can pick it up.

  In the outer thread body (line 175-179)

  Right now it's while (true) { /* await while states empty */ } with no body. The intended loop body is:

  1. statesQueue.take() (blocks until at least one State arrives) — but the result isn't used directly; it just gates the cycle start. (Or you put it back into the queue or hold it as currentFirst.)
  2. Reset cycle state: index = 0 (and Arrays.fill(array, (byte) 0) if you want to be tidy).
  3. upstreamPublisher.subscribe(this) — synchronously triggers this.onSubscribe(s) which stores upstreamSubscription and could s.request(HelloWorld.BYTES) (or you can request from inside this same outer-loop block, after
  subscribe returns).
  4. Wait for the cycle to complete — usually via a per-cycle CountDownLatch countdown that happens at the end of onNext when index == BYTES (or in onError).
  5. Loop back.

  In onNext(b) (lines 194-202) — TODOs

  1. upstreamSubscription.cancel() — cancel the subscription, since we got our 12 bytes.
  2. Drain statesQueue into a local list of recipients (including the "first" you held when you woke up, if you removed it from the queue).
  3. For each recipient (skip cancelled): copy array contents into state.queue and signal(state) to wake the worker.
  4. Clear/reset for next cycle: index = 0 (and signal the cycle-done latch so the outer thread can loop).

  Stale code from the old design (still present, will conflict)

  These are the old design's leftovers and should be removed once the new design works:

  - Constructor line 100: this.fetcher = Thread.ofVirtual().start(this::runFetcher); — eager start. New design wants lazy via outer. Remove this and the fetcher field at the bottom.
  - Methods runFetcher() (105-112), runCycle(...) (114-143), distribute(...) (145-156) — these implement the old "inner anonymous Subscriber per cycle" approach. They use statesQueue too, so leaving both fetcher and outer
  running would have two threads racing for statesQueue.take(). Remove these methods.
  - onError(t) and onComplete() still throw UnsupportedOperationException (lines 208-218). In the new design they should be real code: onError(t) propagates t to all current recipients via terminalError; onComplete() is a
  no-op (already distributed on the 12th onNext).

  Spec & threading caveats to be aware of

  1. Rule 2.12 (multi-cycle): if outer loops and subscribes this to upstreamPublisher more than once, this.onSubscribe(s) is called multiple times. Reactive Streams spec says a Subscriber must call subscription.cancel() if
   its onSubscribe is called more than once. ReactiveHelloWorldBytePublisher doesn't enforce this, so it works in practice — but it's a known caveat to call out in the class doc.
  2. Memory visibility of array and index: they're accessed by the upstream producer thread inside onNext. With cycle boundaries clean (one cycle's onNext completes before the next cycle's upstreamPublisher.subscribe(this)
   starts), they don't need to be volatile — the subscribe/onSubscribe happens-before chain handles it. But it's worth noting in a comment.
  3. State.demand access: the per-subscriber worker thread reads/writes it; the downstream's subscription.request(n) thread also writes it. AtomicLong is already there, so that's fine.

  Want me to fill in the blanks?

  If you'd like, I can:
  - Option 1: leave your sketch alone and just write a # changes needed checklist matching the gaps above so you can fill them in yourself.
  - Option 2: take the gaps above and write the full implementation, replacing the stale runFetcher/runCycle/distribute code with the new outer-thread design.

  Which?

      25 + * onSubscribe}, {@link #onNext(Byte) onNext}, {@link #onError(Throwable) onError}, and
      26 + * {@link #onComplete() onComplete} are real, executed code (not dead).
      27 + * <p>
      28 + * Three concurrent moving parts:
      29   * <ol>
      27 - *   <li>A shared, long-running <strong>fetcher</strong> virtual thread that drives a single
      28 - *       {@link ReactiveHelloWorldBytePublisher} instance. The fetcher blocks on a
      29 - *       {@link BlockingQueue}{@code <State>} of pending subscribers; when at least one is waiting,
      30 - *       it subscribes to the byte publisher, {@code request(12)}, accumulates the bytes into a
      31 - *       local buffer, and on the <em>last</em> {@code onNext} drains the pending queue and pushes
      32 - *       the 12-byte payload into each pending subscriber's per-subscriber queue. Then it loops
      33 - *       back and blocks again for the next "first subscriber".</li>
      34 - *   <li>One <strong>worker</strong> virtual thread per downstream subscriber. The worker, started
      35 - *       by {@link #subscribe(Subscriber)}, first offers its {@code State} to the pending queue
      36 - *       (waking the fetcher), then loops on its own {@link BlockingQueue}{@code <Byte>}, draining
      37 - *       it into {@code downstream.onNext(...)} as the downstream's demand permits.</li>
      38 - *   <li>The producer virtual thread <em>inside</em> the byte publisher, which emits the 12 bytes

  ⎿  (timeout 2m)

⏺ Compiles cleanly. Summary of what's now in place per your sketch:

  Subscriber side — REAL code (no more UnsupportedOperationException)

  - onSubscribe(s) — stores upstreamSubscription = s; calls s.request(BYTES).
  - onNext(b) — array[index++] = b; on the 12th byte:
    a. upstreamSubscription.cancel() (your TODO),
    b. distribute() — drain statesQueue into a recipients list (including currentFirst), push the 12 bytes into each state's queue, signal each worker,
    c. reset index and currentFirst,
    d. cycleDone.countDown() so the outer thread loops back.
  - onError(t) — drains statesQueue + currentFirst, sets terminalError on each, signals each worker; resets cycle; counts down.
  - onComplete() — no-op (distribution already happened on the 12th onNext).

  Publisher side

  - subscribe(downstream) — non-blocking:
    a. validates,
    b. creates a State,
    c. starts a per-subscriber worker virtual thread (which calls downstream.onSubscribe, offers state to statesQueue, then loops on its own queue),
    d. lazily starts the single outer virtual thread under synchronized(this).

  Outer thread loop (runOuter)

  loop forever:
      first = statesQueue.take()      // blocks until first subscriber waiting
      currentFirst = first
      index = 0
      cycleDone = new CountDownLatch(1)
      upstreamPublisher.subscribe(this)   // synchronously calls this.onSubscribe → request(12)
                                          // upstream's producer thread then emits onNext × 12
      cycleDone.await()                   // unblocked by onNext (12th) or onError

  Worker loop (runWorker)

  downstream.onSubscribe(subscription {
      request(n): demand += n; signal;
      cancel():   cancelled = true; signal;
  })
  statesQueue.offer(state)            // wake outer
  loop:
      wait until cancelled OR (demand > 0 AND queue not empty) OR terminalError
      if cancelled:       return
      if terminalError:   downstream.onError(t); return
      demand--; downstream.onNext(queue.poll())
      if delivered == 12: downstream.onComplete(); return

  Stale code removed

  - The old eagerly-started fetcher field and Thread.ofVirtual().start(this::runFetcher) in the constructor.
  - The old runFetcher() / runCycle() methods (which used an inner anonymous Subscriber<Byte> per cycle).
  - array, index, currentFirst, cycleDone are now real cycle-state fields used by onNext / onError.

  Class doc updated

  Reflects the new design: this is the byte-publisher's Subscriber, outer is lazily started, multi-cycle. The Rule 2.12 caveat (subscribing this more than once across cycles) is documented as a known spec deviation.

✻ Baked for 2m 25s

  ⎿  WARNING: A terminally deprecated method in sun.misc.Unsafe has been called
     WARNING: sun.misc.Unsafe::objectFieldOffset has been called by lombok.permit.Permit

  ⎿  (timeout 2m)

⏺ Compiles cleanly. Summary of the inlining:

  All four private helpers — distribute(), runOuter(), runWorker(...), and signal(...) — are gone. Their bodies are now inline at every callsite within the public methods:

  ┌──────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │    Public method     │                                                What's inline now                                                │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ onNext(Byte b)                      │ the distribute body (drain statesQueue + push array to each state's queue) and the signal body (lock+signalAll)                                                                 │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ onError(Throwable t)                │ the same drain-and-signal-error sequence, signal body inlined                                                                                                                   │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ subscribe(Subscriber<? super Byte>  │ both the per-subscriber runWorker (as a Thread.ofVirtual().start(() -> { ... }) block) and the lazy runOuter (as a second Thread.ofVirtual().start(() -> { ... }) block under   │
  │ downstream)                         │ synchronized(this)); the signal body is inlined inside the worker's request/cancel and inside the delivery loop too                                                             │
  └─────────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  onSubscribe(s) and onComplete() didn't call any helpers, so they're untouched.

  The class is now five public methods plus fields plus the private State record-like inner class — every behavioural piece is visible directly inside whichever public method owns it. Each // ---- inlined ... ---- comment
  marks a chunk so a reader can match it to its original helper.

  Want me to also inline the State inner class into something (it's currently a nested type), or leave it as the only piece of structure outside the public methods?

✻ Brewed for 1m 17s

❯ what's currentFirst?

⏺ currentFirst is the bookkeeping field for the one State that the outer thread already took out of statesQueue but still needs to be included in this cycle's distribution.

  Why it exists

  The outer thread uses statesQueue.take() to block until at least one subscriber is waiting:

  final State first;
  try { first = statesQueue.take(); }      // ← REMOVES first from queue
  catch (...) { ... }
  currentFirst = first;

  take() is the blocking primitive — it returns when the queue has ≥ 1 element, and removes that element. So after the outer wakes up:

  before take():   queue = [S1, S2, S3]     ← three subscribers waiting
  after take():    first = S1
                   queue = [S2, S3]         ← S1 is gone from the queue

  Now the outer kicks off a cycle. While the cycle is running, more subscribers might join — say S4, S5 — and the queue becomes [S2, S3, S4, S5].

  When distribute() fires on the 12th onNext, it needs to push the 12-byte payload to all four recipients: S1, S2, S3, S4, S5… wait, all five: S1 plus the four still in the queue. But statesQueue.drainTo(recipients) only
  sees [S2, S3, S4, S5] — S1 was already removed.

  That's why distribute does this:

  if (currentFirst != null) recipients.add(currentFirst);    // ← put S1 back in by hand
  statesQueue.drainTo(recipients);                            // ← drain S2, S3, S4, S5


  if (currentFirst != null) recipients.add(currentFirst);    // ← put S1 back in by hand
  statesQueue.drainTo(recipients);                            // ← drain S2, S3, S4, S5

  currentFirst is the field that holds S1 between when the outer took it and when distribute uses it.

  When it's reset

  After distribute pushes bytes to all recipients, onNext sets currentFirst = null so the next cycle starts clean. If onError fires instead, it does the same reset.

  Could we avoid it?

  Yes — if we used a different "first subscriber arrived" signal that doesn't remove items from the queue. Two options:

  1. A Semaphore for "subscriber count" that the offering thread release()s and the outer acquire()s. Subscribers stay in the queue until drained.
  2. A Condition on a separate lock signalled by the offer side and awaited by the outer.

  Either replaces take() and lets distribute()'s single drainTo pick up everyone — no currentFirst field needed. Cleaner, but adds a separate sync primitive.

  With the current BlockingQueue<State>.take() design, currentFirst is the natural cost.

✻ Crunched for 22s
❯ I don't get it. we drain all states which pushed so far

⏺ Two things to put on the table:

  Where currentFirst comes from

  It's not the design's intent — it's a side effect of statesQueue.take(). Look at runOuter line 195:

  final State first;
  try { first = statesQueue.take(); }

  take() blocks until ≥ 1 element AND removes that element. There is no Java-built-in "block until non-empty without removing". So after the outer wakes up:

  before take():   queue = [S1]            ← worker thread pushed S1, outer was blocked
  take() returns:  first  = S1
                   queue  = []             ← S1 has been REMOVED from the queue

  Now while the cycle is running, S2, S3, S4 might arrive via the short-lived offer threads. Queue becomes [S2, S3, S4].

  At distribute time, statesQueue.drainTo(recipients) gives us [S2, S3, S4]. S1 isn't in the queue anymore. That's why we held it in currentFirst and added it back to recipients by hand.

  So your reading — "drain all states pushed so far" — is what we want, and currentFirst is the patch that makes it work despite take() having yanked one out.

  The cleaner refactor — get rid of currentFirst

  Use a non-removing wait. The simplest version: a Semaphore (or a ReentrantLock + Condition) just for the "subscriber arrived" signal, and keep statesQueue purely as storage:

  private final BlockingQueue<State> statesQueue = new LinkedBlockingQueue<>();
  private final Semaphore subscriberArrived = new Semaphore(0);

  // in subscribe()'s short-lived thread:
  statesQueue.offer(state);
  subscriberArrived.release();         // wake outer

  // in runOuter:
  while (true) {
      subscriberArrived.acquire();      // block until ≥ 1 release (no element removed)
      subscriberArrived.drainPermits(); // collapse pending wake-ups for this cycle
      // statesQueue still has all states; cycle runs; distribute drains everything
      index = 0;
      cycleDone = new CountDownLatch(1);
      upstreamPublisher.subscribe(this);
      cycleDone.await();
  }

  // in distribute():
  final var recipients = new ArrayList<State>();
  statesQueue.drainTo(recipients);     // ← gets EVERYONE, including the "first"
  // (currentFirst field is gone)

  ┌────────┬─────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────┐
  │ Aspect │ Current (with currentFirst) │ Refactored (Semaphore) │
  ├─────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Outer waits via    │ statesQueue.take() — removes 1                   │ subscriberArrived.acquire() — removes a permit, not a State │
  ├────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ distribute's drain │ gets all except the first, which we add manually │ gets all, period                                                     │
  ├────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Extra field           │ volatile @Nullable State currentFirst            │ (none) — just a Semaphore instead of Thread.currentFirst bookkeeping                                                             │
  ├───────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Race during late join │ the held currentFirst covers it                  │ new arrivals just offer+release; if they arrive after drainPermits() but before drain, they're still in the queue for this cycle │
  └───────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Want me to apply this refactor and delete currentFirst?

✻ Churned for 6s

❯ no the whole picture!

⏺ 1. Each subscribe(downstream) creates a State (with its own BlockingQueue<Byte>, demand, cancelled flag), starts a worker virtual thread for that state, lazily starts the single outer virtual thread, and fires a
  short-lived thread that pushes the state into statesQueue and notifies the outer.
  2. The worker thread calls downstream.onSubscribe(...), then loops on its own queue, delivering bytes to downstream as request(n) allows, and calls onComplete after the 12th delivered (or onError(terminalError) if set).
  3. The outer thread loops: wait while statesQueue is empty (via synchronized + wait), then reset cycle state and upstreamPublisher.subscribe(this), then await cycleDone.
  4. The processor itself is the Subscriber to the upstream: onSubscribe stores the subscription and request(12); onNext accumulates into a 12-byte buffer; on the 12th byte it cancels the subscription, drains all states
  from statesQueue, pushes the 12 bytes into each state's per-subscriber queue, signals each worker, and counts down cycleDone; onError drains and signals all with the error; onComplete is a no-op.

✻ Brewed for 13s

❯ so our byteprocessor is a real processor, right?

✻ Cooked for 13s

❯ so our byteprocessor is a real processor, right?

⏺ Yes — in this design, ReactiveHelloWorldByteProcessor is a real Processor. Both halves of the interface are doing real work, not nominal:

  - Subscriber side (onSubscribe / onNext / onError / onComplete) — actually invoked. The outer thread calls upstreamPublisher.subscribe(this), so the upstream's producer thread really does call this.onSubscribe(s), then
  this.onNext(b) 12 times. The 12th onNext drives the whole cycle's completion (cancel + drain + distribute + count down).
  - Publisher side (subscribe) — actually invoked by multiple downstream subscribers. Each call adds a State and the processor multiplexes its single upstream stream to all of them.

  That's the whole point of the Processor interface — one instance accepts demand and signals from one upstream and services many downstream subscribers — and this class genuinely does both. The one spec-strictness caveat
  is Rule 2.12 (this gets subscribed more than once, across cycles), but functionally the design uses both halves of the interface as real code.

⏺ Compiles. Final shape:

  runOuter — while(true): synchronized(statesQueue) { while (statesQueue.isEmpty()) statesQueue.wait(); } (non-destructive wait) → index = 0; cycleDone = new CountDownLatch(1); → upstreamPublisher.subscribe(this); →
  cycleDone.await(); → loop.

  Offering thread (in subscribe) — synchronized(statesQueue) { statesQueue.offer(state); statesQueue.notifyAll(); }.

  onSubscribe(s) — store the subscription, s.request(12).

  onNext(b) — array[index++] = b; on the 12th: upstreamSubscription.cancel(); distribute(); cycleDone.countDown();.

  onError(t) — drain everything in statesQueue under synchronized, set terminalError + signal each; cycleDone.countDown().

  onComplete() — no-op.

  distribute() — synchronized(statesQueue) { statesQueue.drainTo(recipients); }; push the 12 bytes into each non-cancelled state's queue and signal it.

  Fields: upstreamPublisher, upstreamSubscription, array, index, cycleDone, statesQueue, outer. currentFirst deleted.

  Per-subscriber worker (in subscribe) unchanged: calls downstream.onSubscribe(subscription), loops on its own state.queue delivering as state.demand allows, terminates after 12 delivered or on cancel/error.

⏺ Compiles. Summary of what's in State#run and the surrounding changes:

  State#run — two phases per the TODOs:

  1. Await while queue is empty (also exits on cancelled or terminalError):
  while (!cancelled && terminalError == null && queue.isEmpty()) workAvailable.awaitUninterruptibly();
  if (cancelled) return; if (terminalError != null) downstream.onError(terminalError); return;
  lock.unlock();
  2. Queue now holds 12 bytes — loop 12 times, each iteration waits for demand > 0 then downstream.onNext(queue.poll()):
  for delivered = 0 .. 11:
      wait for demand > 0 (or cancel)
      demand--; downstream.onNext(queue.poll())
  downstream.onComplete()

  State#subscription() — returns the Subscription given to downstream; request(n) and cancel() both touch state fields directly and call signal().

  State#signal() — instance method (was static on the processor).

  Processor#subscribe — now does downstream.onSubscribe(state.subscription()) on the caller's thread (before any worker thread runs), then Thread.ofVirtual().start(state) (using State itself as the Runnable), then the
  lazy-outer + short-lived-offer threads as before.

  Removed: the standalone runWorker(State state) method and the static signal(State). onError and distribute now call state.signal() (instance method).

✻ Cogitated for 26s

❯ what are you doing from 140?

  Read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ What's on lines 140-153 (the per-byte delivery loop after the queue is filled):

  for (int delivered = 0; delivered < HelloWorld.BYTES; delivered++) {
      lock.lock();
      try {
          while (!cancelled.get() && demand.get() == 0L) {
              workAvailable.awaitUninterruptibly();
          }
          if (cancelled.get()) return;
      } finally {
          lock.unlock();
      }
      demand.decrementAndGet();
      downstream.onNext(queue.poll());
  }
  downstream.onComplete();

  Reading it line by line:

  - 140: counted loop, 0…11. Iteration delivers one byte to downstream.
  - 141-149: per-iteration wait — under state.lock, sleep on workAvailable while cancelled is false and demand is 0. So this iteration blocks until the downstream calls request(n) (which bumps demand and signals). If
  cancelled flips during the wait, the worker returns without delivering this byte or any further ones, and without onComplete.
  - 150: outside the lock, atomically decrement demand — we're about to consume one unit of demand for the upcoming onNext.
  - 151: pull the next byte from queue (poll returns the head; since queue.size() == BYTES after distribute and we poll once per iteration, it never returns null under normal flow) and emit it via downstream.onNext.
  - 152: end of iteration; delivered increments.
  - 153: after 12 successful onNext calls, signal the natural end with downstream.onComplete().

  So the loop's invariant is "deliver exactly one byte per iteration, respecting downstream's request(n)-driven demand" with cancel-aware early exit. After all 12 bytes have been delivered, the loop falls through to
  onComplete().

✻ Cooked for 30s

❯ read 153, why we need delivered? just plain while?

⏺ You're right — delivered is redundant. The queue already tells us when we're done: it starts with exactly 12 bytes (asserted at line 138), each iteration polls one, so when the queue is empty we've delivered all 12. The
  counter is just tracking what the queue's own size tracks.

  Plain while form:

  while (!queue.isEmpty()) {
      lock.lock();
      try {
          while (!cancelled.get() && demand.get() == 0L) {
              workAvailable.awaitUninterruptibly();
          }
          if (cancelled.get()) return;
      } finally {
          lock.unlock();
      }
      demand.decrementAndGet();
      downstream.onNext(queue.poll());
  }
  downstream.onComplete();

  Apply it?

✻ Cogitated for 54s

❯ line 104 offer or just put?

  Searched for 1 pattern, read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Line 104 in my view is signal(); inside cancel() — no offer/put there. I suspect you meant one of the two offer calls in the file:

  ┌──────┬────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────┬──────────────┬──────────────┐
  │ Line │                          Call                          │                  Queue                  │   Capacity   │ offer vs put │
  ├──────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ 184  │ state.queue.offer(element) (inside inlined distribute) │ per-subscriber ArrayBlockingQueue<Byte> │ bounded = 12 │ matters      │
  ├──────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ 229  │ statesQueue.offer(state) (short-lived thread)          │ shared LinkedBlockingQueue<State>       │ unbounded    │ identical    │
  └──────┴────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────┴──────────────┴──────────────┘

  For line 229 (unbounded LinkedBlockingQueue): offer never returns false and put never blocks. Same behavior. Pick whichever reads better — I'd say put because the intent is "always enqueue, this must succeed."

  For line 184 (bounded ArrayBlockingQueue<>(12)):
  - offer(e) — non-blocking; returns false if full (we currently ignore the return value).
  - put(e) — blocks if full; throws InterruptedException.

  In our flow, the per-subscriber queue is empty when we enter the distribute block (a freshly created State whose queue we're pushing into for the first time), and we push exactly 12 — the capacity. So offer will always
  succeed and put would never actually block. Functionally equivalent.

  Semantically:
  - offer reads as "best-effort enqueue". Returning false would silently lose a byte if the queue invariant were ever violated.
  - put reads as "must enqueue, wait if necessary". Blocking would be visible as a hang if the invariant were violated.

  put is more "intentional"; offer is more "pragmatic". For a teaching artifact I'd lean put because the intent is "guaranteed to enqueue all 12" and put says that aloud — at the cost of handling InterruptedException.

  Which one would you like at each site?

❯ now analyze the file

⏺ File analysis — ReactiveHelloWorldByteProcessor.java

  Two-level structure

  ┌───────┬─────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Level │              Name               │                                                         Role                                                          │
  ├───────┼─────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Outer │ ReactiveHelloWorldByteProcessor │ implements Processor<Byte, Byte> — Subscriber to one upstream BytePublisher; Publisher to many downstream subscribers │
  ├───────┼─────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Inner │ State implements Runnable       │ per-downstream subscriber bookkeeping + delivery thread body                                                          │
  └───────┴─────────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Threads (three kinds; many instances at runtime)

  ┌─────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │         Thread          │                      Started in                      │              Lifetime               │                                         What it does                                          │
  ├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Worker (per subscriber) │ subscribe() via Thread.ofVirtual().start(state)      │ one per subscribe(), exits after    │ runs State#run — waits for its queue to be filled, then delivers 12 bytes respecting demand   │
  │                         │                                                      │ onComplete / cancel                 │                                                                                               │
  ├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Outer (one per          │ subscribe() under synchronized(this), lazily (first  │ infinite loop (daemon)              │ each iteration: synchronized(queue) wait() until non-empty → index=0; latch = new             │
  │ processor)              │ subscribe only)                                      │                                     │ CountDownLatch(1); publisher.subscribe(this); latch.await()                                   │
  ├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Offer-helper (one per   │ subscribe() (last)                                   │ short-lived, exits after one offer  │ synchronized(queue) { queue.offer(state); queue.notifyAll(); } — wakes outer                  │
  │ subscribe)              │                                                      │                                     │                                                                                               │
  ├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ (upstream's own         │ inside publisher.subscribe(this)                     │ one per cycle                       │ emits 12 bytes via this.onNext then exits                                                     │
  │ producer)               │                                                      │                                     │                                                                                               │
  └─────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  State (per-subscriber inner class)

  - Fields — downstream (private), queue (cap 12, package), demand (private), terminated (package), lock (private), condition (private). Only queue and terminated are touched by outer.
  - subscription() — the Subscription the worker hands to downstream.onSubscribe(...). request(n) increments demand and signals; cancel() sets terminated and signals.
  - signal() — lock.lock(); condition.signalAll(); lock.unlock();
  - run() — two phases:
    a. Wait while !terminated && queue.isEmpty(). (Filled by the outer's onNext distribute step.)
    b. While !queue.isEmpty(): wait while !terminated && demand == 0; check terminated; demand--; downstream.onNext(queue.poll()).
    c. downstream.onComplete().

  Processor (outer class)

  - Subscriber side (called by upstream producer thread):
    - onSubscribe(s) — store subscription, s.request(12).
    - onNext(b) — array[index] = b; if (++index == BYTES) { subscription.cancel(); queue.drainTo(drained); for each state: skip if terminated, push 12 bytes into state.queue, state.signal(); latch.countDown(); }.
    - onError(t) — empty.
    - onComplete() — empty.
  - Publisher side (called by external callers):
    - onError(t) — empty.
    - onComplete() — empty.
  - Publisher side (called by external callers):
    - subscribe(downstream):
        i. Create State.
      ii. downstream.onSubscribe(state.subscription()) (synchronous, on caller's thread).
      iii. Start worker via Thread.ofVirtual().start(state).
      iv. synchronized(this) lazily start outer.
      v. Short-lived Thread.ofVirtual().start(...) that does synchronized(queue) { queue.offer(state); queue.notifyAll(); }.

  Cycle flow (the happy path)

  caller        worker          offer-helper                   outer                    upstream
  subscribe →
                start
                                start
                                                               (lazy start)
                onSubscribe→
                wait queue
                                sync queue
                                offer state
                                notifyAll →
                                                               wakes from queue.wait()
                                                               index=0; latch=new(1)
                                                               publisher.subscribe(this) →
                                                                                         producer onSubscribe
                                                                                         request(12)
                                                                                         12× onNext
                                                                                         (12th onNext:)
                                                                                           cancel
                                                                                           drainTo(drained)
                                                                                           push 12 bytes → state.queue
                                                                                           state.signal()
                                                                                           latch.countDown()
                wakes from queue.wait()
                loop 12×:
                  wait demand>0
                  onNext(byte)
                onComplete
                                                               latch.await() returns
                                                               loop back to queue.wait()

  Concurrency review

  - Processor.queue (states): LinkedBlockingQueue. offer/notifyAll are wrapped in synchronized(queue) so the outer's wait is coordinated. drainTo in onNext is NOT inside synchronized(queue) — relies on the queue's internal
   thread safety, which is fine for the consumer side.
  - Processor.array/index/subscription/latch: confined to the producer thread per cycle. The outer thread sets index=0; latch=new(1) before calling publisher.subscribe(this); the subscribe call provides a happens-before
  edge. ✓ for visibility.
  - State.queue: filled by upstream producer thread (under no lock, just offer), drained by worker (calls poll). ArrayBlockingQueue is internally thread-safe. ✓
  - State.demand: atomic; modified by subscription's request (downstream's thread) and by worker (delivery loop). ✓
  - State.terminated: atomic; read in distribute, worker, and subscription. ✓
  - State.lock + condition: used by worker (await), subscription (signal), and distribute (state.signal()). Standard ReentrantLock pattern. ✓

  Smells / issues

  1. Stale class doc:
    - Lines 30-53 say statesQueue.take(), but the field is now queue and the mechanism is wait/notify, not take.
    - "worker… offers its State onto statesQueue" — the offer-helper does that now, not the worker.
  2. Name clash: both Processor.queue (states) and State.queue (bytes) carry the same name. Disambiguated only by state.queue vs queue. Was clearer when statesQueue was named that.
  3. Class doc mentions "the worker thread" doing onSubscribe (line 56-57). With the latest refactor, downstream.onSubscribe runs on the caller's thread (line 217), not the worker.
  4. No request(n ≤ 0) validation in State#subscription. Spec Rule 3.9 says request(n ≤ 0) must onError. Currently silently adds to demand. If a caller passes 0 or a negative, the assertion demand > 0L in run may not even
  fire, but the underlying contract is broken.
  5. onError / onComplete on the Processor are empty. If upstream errors mid-cycle (onError), the cycle's latch is never counted down → the outer thread hangs forever. Same for onComplete if upstream completes before
  emitting 12 (shouldn't happen for our BytePublisher, but still). You acknowledged this is out-of-scope for now.
  6. No close() / AutoCloseable. The outer thread runs forever. Virtual-thread daemons don't prevent JVM exit, but in long-running JVMs (or test suites that create many processors) the threads accumulate.
  7. Rule 2.12 caveat is documented but real: publisher.subscribe(this) happens every cycle.
  8. assert offered on state.queue.offer(element) assumes the per-subscriber queue starts empty. True for new subscribers entering their first (and only) cycle. Solid invariant given current single-use lifecycle of State.

  Verdict

  The class is genuinely a Processor — both halves of the interface are real, executed code. The cycle-batched multicast pattern works, demand-driven per-subscriber delivery works, late join within a cycle is handled by
  drainTo, late join between cycles triggers the next cycle. The visible loose ends are (a) class doc lagging behind the code, (b) the missing request(n ≤ 0) guard, and (c) the deliberately empty error/complete paths.

  Want me to fix (a) the class doc and/or (b) the request(n) validation?

⏺ Switched both sites from synchronized(queue) + queue.wait/notifyAll to a ReentrantLock + Condition pair:

  - New fields:
    - private final ReentrantLock queueLock = new ReentrantLock();
    - private final Condition queueNotEmpty = queueLock.newCondition();
  - Outer thread's await (inside the inlined run-outer lambda): queueLock.lock(); try { while (queue.isEmpty()) queueNotEmpty.await(); } finally { queueLock.unlock(); }.
  - Offer-helper's signal: queueLock.lock(); try { offer; signalAll(); } finally { queueLock.unlock(); }.

  InterruptedException from queueNotEmpty.await() handled the same way as before (restore interrupt flag, exit thread). Compiles.

⏺ Done. ReactiveHelloWorldByteProcessorState is now fully encapsulated:

  ┌────────────┬─────────┬─────────────────────┐
  │   Field    │ Before  │        After        │
  ├────────────┼─────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ downstream │ private │ private (unchanged) │
  ├────────────┼─────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ queue      │ package │ private             │
  ├────────────┼─────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ demand     │ private │ private (unchanged) │
  ├────────────┼─────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ terminated │ package │ private             │
  ├────────────┼─────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ lock       │ private │ private (unchanged) │
  ├────────────┼─────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ condition  │ private │ private (unchanged) │
  └────────────┴─────────┴─────────────────────┘

  Package-private surface (what the outer Processor can call):
  - subscription() — returns the downstream's Subscription (unchanged).
  - deliver(byte[] payload) — new operation method that:
    a. short-circuits if terminated,
    b. offers every byte of payload (asserts success — queue has exactly enough capacity),
    c. signal()s the worker.

  signal() is now private (no external callers — deliver, subscription()'s request/cancel, and run() are all in-State).

  Processor.onNext simplified from a four-step inline (if (terminated) continue; for (byte) offer; signal) to a one-liner: state.deliver(array);.

  Compiles clean.

⏺ Done. Three artifacts now:

  ReactiveHelloWorldProcessorWorker.java (new) — package-private Runnable. Constructor takes (processor, lock, condition, queue). run() loops:
  1. lock.lock(); try { while (queue.isEmpty()) condition.await(); } finally { lock.unlock(); }
  2. processor.runCycle(); (delegates to Processor for the upstream-subscribe-and-await)

  On InterruptedException in either step: restore interrupt flag and return.

  ReactiveHelloWorldByteProcessor.java — subscribe(...) replaced the inlined lambda with:
  thread = Thread.ofVirtual().start(
          new ReactiveHelloWorldProcessorWorker(this, lock, condition, queue));

  Added a new package-private method:
  void runCycle() throws InterruptedException {
      index = 0;
      latch = new CountDownLatch(1);
      publisher.subscribe(this);
      latch.await();
  }

  This pulls the per-cycle reset + subscribe-this + latch.await sequence into Processor (where the cycle-state fields live), and lets the Worker stay generic — it just gates "wait until non-empty" and triggers runCycle().

  ReactiveHelloWorldByteProcessorState.java — already extracted earlier with private fields + subscription() / deliver(byte[]) / private signal().

  The three-class layout now:

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                Class                 │                                           Owns                                            │                           Knows about                           │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ ReactiveHelloWorldByteProcessor      │ upstream subscription, cycle state (array/index/latch), lock/condition/queue, lazy thread │ upstream publisher, State, Worker                               │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ ReactiveHelloWorldProcessorWorker    │ nothing (just shared references)                                                          │ Processor (calls runCycle), and the shared lock/condition/queue │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ ReactiveHelloWorldByteProcessorState │ per-subscriber downstream/queue/demand/terminated/lock/condition                          │ nothing outside itself                                          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Compiles cleanly.

  Net on Processor: −3 fields.

  Worker now owns the coordination state (lock, condition, queue) and exposes two package-private operations:
  - offer(State state) — lock.lock(); offer + signalAll; lock.unlock();
  - drainTo(Collection sink) — delegates to the queue (no lock — concurrent-safe drain by the consumer side)

  Processor changes:
  - subscribe(...) lazily creates worker = new ReactiveHelloWorldByteProcessorWorker(this); Thread.ofVirtual().start(worker); (under synchronized(this)); the short-lived offer thread now just calls worker.offer(state).
  - onNext(...) now does worker.drainTo(drained); instead of manipulating its own queue.

❯ I just moved state and worker into procesosr,

⏺ Looking through the file holistically:

  Field placement — already well-distributed:
  - State privately owns its own downstream/queue/demand/terminated/lock/condition (only State touches them).
  - Worker privately owns processor/lock/condition/queue (only Worker + the enclosing Processor touch them).
  - Processor owns publisher/worker/subscription/array/index/latch.

  Since State and Worker are nested static classes, all fields can stay private — nestmate access lets the enclosing class read private fields of its nested classes directly. No need to relax to package-private.

  Methods that can collapse into direct field access:
  - Worker.drainTo(sink) is a pure pass-through to queue.drainTo(sink). Since Processor.onNext has nestmate access to worker.queue, this method earns nothing. Remove and inline.

  Other small optimizations that fall out:
  - Mark State and Worker final (they're already private static class — adding final codifies intent).
  - Use double-checked locking on the lazy worker field (it's already volatile).

  Let me apply.

⏺ Done. Summary of the embed + re-optimize:

  Worker class — fully embedded. Its three duties are now inline:

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │              Old Worker thing               │                Now in Processor as                │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Worker.run (wait + delegate)                │ subscribe(...)'s outer-thread lambda              │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Worker.offer(state) (lock + offer + signal) │ subscribe(...)'s short-lived offer-thread lambda  │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Worker.drainTo(sink)                        │ onNext(...) calls queue.drainTo(drained) directly │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Worker.processor field                      │ gone — we are the processor                       │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Worker.lock / condition / queue             │ moved up to Processor fields                      │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  runCycle() also inlined. Was a one-call-site helper; collapsed into the outer-thread lambda.

  Double-checked locking added on thread initialization (it was already volatile):

  if (thread == null) {
      synchronized (this) {
          if (thread == null) { thread = ... }
      }
  }

  — skips the synchronized hot path once thread is non-null.

  State marked final (it was already private static; final makes intent explicit).

  Unused java.util.Collection import removed.

  Class doc is somewhat stale (still mentions statesQueue.take()/worker offering, etc.). Want me to refresh it now that the structure is settled?

  Final Processor field count: 9 (publisher, lock, condition, queue, thread, subscription, array, index, latch) — same as before the Worker extraction, but the code has been through extract→embed and is now consolidated
  with DCL + final State + inlined runCycle.

⏺ Done. The Subscription anonymous class that used to live behind State.subscription() is now built directly in Processor.subscribe(s):

  s.onSubscribe(new Subscription() {
      @Override public void request(final long n) {
          if (state.terminated.get()) { return; }
          state.demand.addAndGet(n);
          state.signal();
      }
      @Override public void cancel() {
          state.terminated.set(true);
          state.signal();
      }
  });

  The method State.subscription() is removed. The new code reaches into State's terminated, demand, and signal() directly — all private, but that's fine because State is a nested static class of Processor, and Java's
  nestmate access permits the enclosing class to touch private members of its nested classes.

  State.signal() is still needed — it's invoked by State.deliver(...), so it stays as a private method on State.

  Other State surface now:
  - run() (the Runnable body)
  - deliver(byte[]) (the operation method used by onNext)
  - signal() (private — used by deliver internally, and by the inlined Subscription in Processor.subscribe)

  Compiles cleanly.

⏺ Done. State.deliver(byte[]) removed. Its body is inlined into the loop in Processor.onNext:

  for (final var state : drained) {
      if (state.terminated.get()) {
          continue;                                           // was `return` inside deliver
      }
      for (final byte element : array) {
          final boolean offered = state.queue.offer(element);
          assert offered;
      }
      state.signal();
  }

  (return becomes continue since we're inside a loop now, not the standalone method.) The inner-byte variable is element rather than b to avoid shadowing Byte b in the outer onNext(Byte b) parameter.

  State's remaining surface:
  - run() (Runnable body)
  - signal() (private — still called by Processor.onNext via state.signal(), valid nestmate access)

  Compiles cleanly.

  Empty body. Reachable in theory (e.g., if upstream's service.set(...) throws — recall we removed the try-catch around it in BytePublisher earlier). If it ever fires:
  - the latch is never counted down,
  - outer thread hangs forever on latch.await(),
  - pending subscribers never see anything.

  This is the "error handling is out of scope" you flagged. Not currently safe in the presence of upstream errors.

  body is still fine because latch is already counted down by onNext.

  @Nullable on thread, subscription, latch reflects pre-init nullness, accurate.

  Cross-cutting issues (genuinely substantive)

  1. request(n ≤ 0) not validated (line 194-198) — Rule 3.9 violation. Fix: if (n <= 0L) { downstream.onError(new IllegalArgumentException("non-positive request: " + n)); return; }.
  2. onError(t) empty — if upstream errors, the outer thread hangs on latch.await() forever and subscribers see nothing. Need: at minimum, propagate t to all subscribers in queue and latch.countDown().
  3. subscription.cancel() on line 153 is redundant — BytePublisher would call our (empty) onComplete naturally. Removing it has no functional effect.
  4. Short-lived offer thread on lines 233-240 is unneeded — the work it does is non-blocking. Could be inlined to remove one Thread.ofVirtual().start(...) per subscribe call (cheap, but unnecessary).
  5. publisher.subscribe(this) on line 222 — called every cycle. Rule 2.12 forbids subscribing the same Subscriber more than once. BytePublisher doesn't enforce. Already documented as a known caveat.
  6. No shutdown / close — outer thread loops forever. Virtual daemon, so JVM-exit-safe, but in long-running tests the thread accumulates.
  7. Stale class javadoc (lines 19-65) — references statesQueue.take(), "worker offers", etc. None of those match the current code. Will mislead a reader who only reads the doc.

  Net summary

  Truly required everywhere it appears, no waste:
  - State class in its entirety
  - onSubscribe, the body of onNext minus subscription.cancel(), all fields
  - subscribe's null check, State creation, downstream.onSubscribe, worker start, DCL outer thread

  Removable without changing observable behavior:
  - subscription.cancel(); on line 153
  - The Thread.ofVirtual().start(() -> { ... }) wrapper on lines 233-240 (inline the body)

  Missing for correctness/spec:
  - request(n ≤ 0) validation in the inline Subscription
  - onError(t) propagation (out of scope per your earlier note, but it's the largest functional gap)

✻ Crunched for 2m 0s

❯ line 204, is it ok to start the thread after call onSubscribe?

⏺ Yes, the current order is correct. In fact it's the safer of the two possible orders. Let me walk through the reasoning.

  The sequence with current order

  Thread A (subscribe caller):
      new State(s)
      s.onSubscribe(subscription)        // (1) downstream gets Subscription
      Thread.ofVirtual().start(state)    // (2) worker thread B begins
      [...lazy outer + offer thread...]

  Thread B (worker, State.run):
      lock.lock()
      while (queue.isEmpty()) condition.awaitUninterruptibly();
      ...

  The two orderings differ only in whether (1) happens before (2). Both are functionally safe — but (1)→(2) is preferable.

  Why (1)→(2) is correct

  1. Reactive Streams Rule 1.9 says Publisher.subscribe MUST call onSubscribe on the provided Subscriber prior to any other signals. Worker (Thread B) is the only path that emits onNext/onComplete to s. So onSubscribe (on
  Thread A) must finish before Thread B emits anything. Current order guarantees this trivially.
  2. Memory visibility is not a concern. Thread.start(state) is a synchronization action — all writes Thread A made before start are visible to Thread B. So even if downstream calls subscription.request(n) synchronously
  inside s.onSubscribe on Thread A (which races nothing because it just touches state.demand and state.signal()), Thread B will see the updated demand when it acquires the lock.
  3. The "lost signal" question is also non-issue. If downstream calls request(n) inside onSubscribe, our subscription does demand.addAndGet(n); signal();. The signal() acquires the lock and signalAll()s a Condition with
  no waiters yet — that's not a bug; it's defined as a no-op for missing waiters. When Thread B later enters the lock, its while (!terminated && queue.isEmpty()) check re-evaluates state. Since queue is still empty
  (distribute hasn't run yet), B correctly waits. Eventually distribute fills the queue and signals, B wakes. The lost early-signal cost us nothing because the predicate queue.isEmpty() was still true at the missed signal
  moment.

  Why (1)→(2) is better than (2)→(1)

  If we instead did start(state) first and s.onSubscribe(...) second, and s.onSubscribe(...) threw, you'd have:
  - worker thread alive, parked on condition.awaitUninterruptibly() forever
  - subscribe() unwinding with the exception
  - the offer thread (line 233-240) never enqueues the state
  - a permanent orphan thread holding a reference to your processor

  With the current order (onSubscribe first), if it throws, subscribe() unwinds before the worker is even started — no orphan thread.

  Tiny remaining concern (not a bug)

  If s.onSubscribe(...) is slow (downstream does heavy work inside it), the worker isn't started yet — but the worker would have nothing to do anyway (queue empty), so the delay is harmless. Same applies to the lazy outer
  init and the short-lived offer at the end of subscribe().

  So: yes, (1)→(2) is the right order, and the code as-is is correct on this point.

✻ Brewed for 1m 15s

❯ right onSubscribe as a thread, now re-analyze with the line 204, order

⏺ Good question to push on. If we move s.onSubscribe(...) off the caller's thread, there are two ways to do it, and one of them is broken.

  Option A — onSubscribe on its own separate virtual thread

  Thread.ofVirtual().start(() -> s.onSubscribe(new Subscription() { ... }));  // Thread C
  Thread.ofVirtual().start(state);                                              // Thread B

  The two threads are independent. Now the order on line 204 (start B) vs. the new "start C" doesn't matter for the start itself — but the completion order matters for Rule 1.3 / 1.9.

  The race

  The worker thread B's path to downstream.onNext(...):

  1. state is added to queue (by the short-lived offer thread).
  2. Outer thread wakes, calls publisher.subscribe(this), the upstream emits 12 onNext, Processor.onNext distributes, fills state.queue, signals B.
  3. B wakes from phase 1, enters phase 2, waits for demand > 0, then calls downstream.onNext(...).

  For downstream.onNext(...) to happen, demand > 0. Demand becomes > 0 only when downstream calls subscription.request(n). Downstream can only call request(n) if it has the Subscription, which it received via
  s.onSubscribe(subscription). So far, nothing wrong: downstream had to receive onSubscribe before being able to bump demand, so onNext can never strictly precede onSubscribe.

  But Rule 1.3 is concurrent invocation, not just ordering of "started":

  ▎ onSubscribe, onNext, onError, and onComplete signalled to a Subscriber MUST be signaled serially (no parallel notifications).

  Imagine downstream's onSubscribe(s) handler calls s.request(12) synchronously and then does a heavy thing for a few seconds. Timeline:

  Thread C:  s.onSubscribe → downstream handler → subscription.request(12) ┐
                                                                            │  parallel
  Thread B:  ... lock+await ... wakes ... downstream.onNext(b) ←────────────┘

  - Downstream is inside s.onSubscribe, on Thread C.
  - B is also invoking s.onNext on Thread B.
  - Two notifications to s are happening in parallel. Rule 1.3 violation.

  There's no order-of-start() that fixes this — even if you start C before B, or B before C, the issue is that C's call to s.onSubscribe may not have returned by the time B starts emitting onNext.

  To repair Option A

  You'd need an explicit synchronization point: e.g., a CountDownLatch onSubscribed = new CountDownLatch(1) that C counts down after s.onSubscribe returns, and B awaits before its first onNext. That makes B's "before first
   onNext" gate explicit:

  // Thread C:
  s.onSubscribe(subscription);
  onSubscribed.countDown();

  // Thread B (somewhere before the first onNext in State.run):
  onSubscribed.await();

  Functionally correct, but you've added a third sync primitive (latch) just to mimic what the current code's call-order achieves for free.

  Option B — onSubscribe inside the worker thread (no separate thread)

  Move the call into the worker:

  // Processor.subscribe:
  Thread.ofVirtual().start(state);   // line 204 equivalent — but now this thread also does onSubscribe

  // State.run (first thing):
  @Override
  public void run() {
      downstream.onSubscribe(subscription);   // happens on Thread B, first
      // ... phase 1 wait, phase 2 delivery
  }

  Now there's only one thread (B) doing signals to downstream — onSubscribe, then onNext × n, then onComplete. They are sequential by construction.

  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                      Concern                      │                                                Status                                                │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Rule 1.3 (serial signals)                         │ trivially satisfied — same thread emits all signals in source order                                  │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Rule 1.9 (onSubscribe first)                      │ trivially satisfied — run calls it before anything else                                              │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ subscribe() blocking on slow onSubscribe?         │ No — caller's thread just does Thread.start() and returns                                            │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Order vs line 204 / DCL outer / short-lived offer │ They run in parallel with B's onSubscribe — but they don't emit signals to s, so no Rule 1.3 concern │
  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  One subtle regression vs. the current design

  In the current code, if downstream.onSubscribe(...) throws, the exception propagates out of subscribe() on the caller's thread before the worker is even started — clean: no orphan thread, no state in queue.

  With Option B, the worker is already running when it calls onSubscribe. If that throws inside State.run, the worker dies abnormally; the state may have already been added to queue by the offer thread; distribute will
  still push 12 bytes into its dead queue; the bytes sit there until GC. Minor leak per failure. A try { ... } catch (Throwable t) { ... } in run could clean up.

  So the answer to "is the order on line 204 OK?"

  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                     Design                     │                         Order on line 204                         │                                             Outcome                                             │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Current (onSubscribe on caller's thread, then  │ onSubscribe before line 204                                       │ Correct. Subsequent worker emissions trivially serialize after onSubscribe; if onSubscribe      │
  │ Thread.start(state))                           │                                                                   │ throws, worker is never started.                                                                │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Option A (onSubscribe on its own separate      │ Either order works for starting, but C's completion may race with │ Broken without an extra CountDownLatch. The order of start() doesn't help.                      │
  │ thread)                                        │  B's signals                                                      │                                                                                                 │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Option B (onSubscribe moved into worker)       │ Worker's run() calls s.onSubscribe(...) as its first statement;   │ Correct. Same single-thread serialization as the current design, just with the "kick" happening │
  │                                                │ subscribe() just Thread.start(state)                              │  one step later. Caller's thread is fully fire-and-forget.                                      │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  If you want onSubscribe to be non-blocking from the caller's perspective, Option B is the right move — and then line 204's relative position is irrelevant because no other thread is producing signals to s. Option A —
  onSubscribe on its own thread separate from the worker — needs an extra latch to be spec-correct.

⏺ Right — sorry, that was me over-spec-lawyering an educational artifact. In the "we trust both sides, focus on the workflow" framing:

  Line 204's order (s.onSubscribe(...) first, then Thread.ofVirtual().start(state)) is fine. Full stop.

  Trusted downstream → onSubscribe returns quickly and doesn't do anything weird synchronously. No race to worry about. Either order would work; the current order is just a tiny bit cleaner because if onSubscribe throws
  (which we trust won't), no orphan worker thread.

  Moving onSubscribe to its own thread is unnecessary for the same reason — we trust downstream not to block in its handler. The current synchronous call is the simplest thing that demonstrates the workflow.

❯ wait why not sync for lazy worker thread? we trust up/downstream not the logic.. multiple subscibers may subscribe concurrently

  we only trust up/downstream!!!! fuck!!!!

⏺ You're right — apologies. "Trust upstream/downstream" ≠ "trust callers". A Publisher.subscribe(...) can be invoked concurrently from many caller threads (or even racing with itself for the very first subscribe), so the
  synchronized(this) DCL around the lazy thread init is required to prevent two outer threads from being started.

  Let me re-list the prior "NOT required" items with that correction:

  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                         Item                          │ Previously called  │                                                             Correct verdict                                                              │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ synchronized(this) { if (thread == null) ... } DCL on │ "Not required      │ Required. Concurrent first-time subscribe(...) calls can race; without DCL we'd risk starting two outer threads, each driving its own    │
  │  lazy outer-thread init                               │ under trust"       │ cycle on the shared publisher / queue. The DCL stays.                                                                                    │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ state.demand, state.terminated being Atomic*          │ (not commented)    │ Required. Subscriber's Subscription.request(n) and cancel() are called from caller threads concurrently with the worker thread; atomic   │
  │                                                       │                    │ types serialize the cross-thread access.                                                                                                 │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ lock/condition/queue coordination between subscribe   │ (not commented)    │ Required. Two callers' subscribes can race with one another and with the outer thread's await/wake. Lock-condition is what makes those   │
  │ (offer side) and outer (await side)                   │                    │ races safe.                                                                                                                              │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ volatile on thread and latch                          │ (not commented)    │ Required. thread is read by the DCL fast-path on caller threads, written by the slow-path under synchronized. latch is written by the    │
  │                                                       │                    │ outer thread, read by onNext on the upstream producer thread — different threads, volatile-needed for visibility.                        │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ volatile on subscription                              │ (not commented)    │ Not required under our trust model — both onSubscribe(s) and onNext(b) (where subscription.cancel() happens) run on the same upstream    │
  │                                                       │                    │ producer thread (per cycle). Trust = upstream serializes its own signals. No cross-thread access.                                        │
  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  contention. Inlining onto the caller's thread is equivalent. Still removable.
